59
) showed that as
\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-modules, one has

\displaystyle[\mathrm{OS}(U_{2,n})_{0}]

\displaystyle\cong[\mathcal{S}^{(n)}],

\displaystyle[\mathrm{OS}(U_{2,n})_{1}]

\displaystyle\cong[\mathcal{S}^{(n)}]+[\mathcal{S}^{(n-1,1)}],

\displaystyle[\mathrm{OS}(U_{2,n})_{2}]

\displaystyle\cong[\mathcal{S}^{(n-1,1)}],

which are representation stable past
n=2
.
Consequently, applying Corollary
10.6
(ii) with
c=2
implies that the Koszul duals

\{\mathrm{OS}(U_{2,n})^{!}_{i}\}
, which are the
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-permutation representations discussed in Proposition
6.1
,
should be representation stable past
n=2i
. In fact, one has the following.

Proposition 10.8
.

For
i\geq 0
, representation stability
of
\{\mathrm{OS}(U_{2,n})^{!}_{i}\}

starts exactly at
n=2i
.

Proof.

Recall that
Proposition
6.1
expresses
[\mathrm{OS}(U_{2,n})^{!}_{i}]

as a nonnegative combination
of classes
\varphi_{(n-d,1^{d})}
for various
d

in the range
2\leq d\leq i
, where
\varphi_{\lambda}
is the class
of the coset representation
\mathbb{k}[\mathfrak{S}_{n}/\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}]
.
Furthermore, the coefficient
on
\varphi_{(n-i,1^{i})}
is
1
.
Since one can write

\varphi_{(n-d,1^{d})}=\left[\left(\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{d}\right)\uparrow_{\mathfrak{S}_{d}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n-d}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\right]\quad\text{ where }\quad\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{d}\cong\bigoplus_{\mu:|\mu|=d}(\mathcal{S}^{\mu})^{\oplus\dim\mathcal{S}^{\mu}},

Lemma
10.5
shows
\{\varphi_{(n-d,1^{d})}\}_{n}

stabilizes exactly at
n=2d
, and

\{[\mathrm{OS}(U_{2,n})^{!}_{i}]\}
exactly at
n=2i
.
∎

Recall from Section
8

that when
A
is Koszul and commutative or anti-commutative,
then
A^{!}=\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{L})
is the universal enveloping algebra for a graded Lie algebra or superalgebra
\mathcal{L}
.
We next show how representation stability of families of Koszul algebras
\{A(n)\}
passes to the Lie (super-)algebras
\{\mathcal{L}(n)\}
.
The following lemma will be useful for this purpose.

Lemma 10.9
.

For any representation stable sequence

\{V_{n}\}
and any partition
\mu
giving rise to a Schur functor
{\mathbb{S}}^{\mu}(-)
, the sequence

\{{\mathbb{S}}^{\mu}(V_{n})\}
is also representation stable.
In particular, for each fixed
m=0,1,2,\ldots
, the
sequences
\{\wedge^{m}(V_{n})\},\{\operatorname{Sym}^{m}(V_{n})\}

are representation stable.

Proof.

Express the representation stable sequence

\{V_{n}\}
for
n\gg 0
as

V_{n}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t}\mathcal{S}^{\lambda^{(i)}[n]},

and proceed by induction on
t
to show

\{{\mathbb{S}}^{\mu}(V_{n})\}
is representation stable for all
\mu
.

The case
t=1
was proven by
Church, Ellenberg, Farb
[
24
, Prop. 3.4.5]

who showed that for any partitions
\lambda,\mu
, the sequence
\{{\mathbb{S}}^{\mu}(\mathcal{S}^{\lambda[n]})\}
is representation stable. In the inductive step,
write

V_{n}=U_{n}\oplus\mathcal{S}^{\lambda^{(i)}[n]}

for
n\gg 0
,
where
U_{n}:=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{t-1}\mathcal{S}^{\lambda^{(i)}[n]}
, so that induction applies to the representation stable sequence
\{U_{n}\}
.
Using the general isomorphism (see, e.g.,
[
2
, Thm. II.4.11]
)

{\mathbb{S}}(X\oplus Y)\cong\bigoplus_{\nu\subseteq\mu}{\mathbb{S}}^{\nu}(X)\otimes{\mathbb{S}}^{\mu/\nu}(Y)

one concludes that, for
n\gg 0
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{S}}^{\mu}(V_{n})={\mathbb{S}}^{\mu}(U_{n}\oplus\mathcal{S}^{\lambda[n]})\cong\bigoplus_{\nu\subseteq\mu}{\mathbb{S}}^{\nu}(U_{n})\otimes{\mathbb{S}}^{\mu/\nu}(\mathcal{S}^{\lambda[n]})\cong\bigoplus_{\nu,\mu,\rho}\left({\mathbb{S}}^{\nu}(U_{n})\otimes{\mathbb{S}}^{\rho}(\mathcal{S}^{\lambda[n]})\right)^{\oplus c^{\mu/\nu}_{\rho}}

for some nonnegative integer (Littlewood-Richardson) coefficients
c^{\mu/\nu}_{\rho}
.
By induction on
t
, the sequences
\{{\mathbb{S}}^{\nu}(U_{n})\}
are representation stable, and by the
t=1
case, the same holds for

\{{\mathbb{S}}^{\rho}(\mathcal{S}^{\lambda[n]})\}
.
Hence by Theorem
10.3
,
each summand
\{{\mathbb{S}}^{\nu}(U_{n})\otimes{\mathbb{S}}^{\rho}(\mathcal{S}^{\lambda[n]})\}
on the right side is a representation
stable sequence, and the same holds for the entire direct sum.
∎

We now apply this to the sequences of
Lie (super-)algebras
\{\mathcal{L}(n)\}
.

Corollary 10.10
.

Let
\{A(n)\}
be a family of Koszul algebras, all commutative (resp. anti-commutative), with
\{\mathcal{L}(n)\}
defined by

A(n)^{!}=\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{L}(n))
. If for
each fixed
i=0,1,2,\ldots
, the sequence
\{A(n)_{i}\}
is representation stable, then for each fixed
i=1,2,\ldots
,
the sequence
\{\mathcal{L}(n)_{i}\}
is also
representation stable.

Proof.

In either case where
\{A(n)\}
are commutative or anti-commutative, use induction on
i
. In the base case
i=1
,
one has this string of equalities, justified below:

[\mathcal{L}(n)_{1}]\overset{(a)}{=}[A(n)^{!}_{1}]\overset{(b)}{=}[\left(A(n)_{1}\right)^{*}]\overset{(c)}{=}[A(n)_{1}].

Equality (a) comes from comparing coefficients of
t^{1}
on either side of
(
79
) or (
76
), equality (b) comes from (
17
), and equality (c) comes from the fact that
\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-modules are all self-contragredient. Since the right sides
\{A(n)_{1}\}
are representation stable, so are the left sides
\{\mathcal{L}(n)_{1}\}
.

In the induction step where
i\geq 2
, rewrite the equalities that come from comparing the coefficient of
t^{i}
on either side of (
79
) or (
76
), isolating the summand
[\mathcal{L}(n)_{i}]
on the right
corresponding to
\lambda=(i)
. For fixed
n
, this expresses
\mathcal{L}(n)_{i}
recursively in terms of

A(n)^{!}_{i}
and
\mathcal{L}(n)_{1},\mathcal{L}(n)_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{L}(n)_{i-1}
:

[\mathcal{L}(n)_{i}]=[A(n)^{!}_{i}]-\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash i:\\
\lambda=(1^{m_{1}}2^{m_{2}}\cdots i^{m_{i}})\\
\lambda\neq(i)\end{subarray}}\prod_{1\leq j<i}[\operatorname{Sym}^{m_{j}}(\mathcal{L}(n)_{j})]\quad\quad\quad\quad\text{ for }A(n)\text{ anti-commutative}.\\
&\\
\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash i:\\
\lambda=(1^{m_{1}}2^{m_{2}}\cdots i^{m_{i}})\\
\lambda\neq(i)\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j<i\\
j\text{ odd }\end{subarray}}[\wedge^{m_{j}}(\mathcal{L}(n)_{j})]\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}2\leq j<i\\
j\text{ even }\end{subarray}}[\operatorname{Sym}^{m_{j}}(\mathcal{L}(n)_{j})]\text{ for }A(n)\text{ commutative}.\end{cases}

Now use Corollary
10.6

asserting that each sequence
\{A(n)^{!}_{i}\}

is representation stable. Induction on
i
shows each sequence
\{\mathcal{L}(n)_{j}\}
for
j\leq i-1
appearing on the right is representation stable.
Lemma
10.9
,
then implies the same for all sequences
\{\wedge^{m_{j}}(\mathcal{L}(n)_{j}\},\{\operatorname{Sym}^{m_{j}}(\mathcal{L}(n)_{j}\}

appearing on the right. Then Theorem
10.3
gives
the same for their tensor products. Thus
every summand on the right is a representation
stable sequence in
n
, and hence so is
\{\mathcal{L}(n)_{i}\}
.
∎

Remark 10.11
.

The above proof shows the following statement for
a sequence of graded Lie (super-)algebras and
\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-modules

\mathcal{L}(n)
, with universal enveloping algebras
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{L}(n))
:
one has for all
i\geq 1
that
\{\mathcal{L}(n)_{i}\}

is representation stable if and only if
one has for all
i\geq 0
that
\{\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{L}(n))_{i}\}

is representation stable.

Remark 10.12
.

Unlike Corollary
10.6
, we have not seriously tried to bound the onset of stability for the sequences
\{\mathcal{L}(n)_{i}\}
, in terms of a given bound for the onset of stability in
\{A(n)_{i}\}
. However,
Sage
computations up to
i=10
suggest the following for uniform matroids
U_{2,n}
of rank
2
.

Conjecture 10.13
.

Defining
\mathcal{L}(n)_{i}
by
\mathrm{OS}(U_{2,n})^{!}=\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{L}(n))
, the sequence
\{\mathcal{L}(n)_{i}\}
is representation stable past
n=2i-1
for fixed
i\geq 3
.

Remark 10.14
.

Although
[A(n)_{i}^{!}]
is a permutation representation when
A(n)=\mathrm{OS}(U_{2,n})
by
Proposition

6.1
, the primitives
[\mathcal{L}(n)_{i}]
are generally
not
classes of permutation representations. This fails immediately for
n=3
and
i=2
, where
[\mathcal{L}(3)_{2}]
is the sign representation.
Also for braid matroids, if
A(n)=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
,
and
A(n)^{!}=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}=\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{L}(n))
, one can check

\mathcal{L}(n)_{2}=(\mathrm{sgn}_{\mathfrak{S}_{3}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{\mathfrak{S}_{n-3}})\big\uparrow^{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}_{\mathfrak{S}_{3}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n-3}}

which is again not a permutation module.

11.
The motivating example: braid matroids and Stirling representations

As mentioned prior to Example
4.7
, our motivation came from the
braid matroids

M=\operatorname{Br}_{n}
, which are
also known as the graphic matroids for complete graphs
K_{n}
. They are also known as the matroids represented by the vectors
\{v_{ij}\}_{1\leq i<j\leq n}
with
v_{ij}:=e_{i}-e_{j}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
which are the (positive) roots in the type
A_{n-1}
root system, whose normal hyperplanes
H_{ij}=\{\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}:x_{i}=x_{j}\}
are the reflecting hyperplanes for the transposition
(i,j)
in the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
when it acts on
V={\mathbb{R}}^{n}

by permuting coordinates. Thus
M=\operatorname{Br}_{n}
is orientable,
and abusing notation slightly, we will also denote by
\mathcal{M}=\operatorname{Br}_{n}
the oriented matroid on the ground set
E=\{\{i,j\}:1\leq i<j\leq n\}

represented by these vectors
\{v_{ij}\}
.

11.1.
Comparison with cohomology of configuration spaces

It turns out that the algebras
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n}),\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
had been studied historically earlier
as the cohomology rings of certain

configuration spaces
of
n
ordered (labeled) points
in a space
X

\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(X):=\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in X^{n}:x_{i}\neq x_{j}\text{ for }1\leq i<j\leq n\}.

The arrangement of hyperplanes
H_{ij}
in
V={\mathbb{R}}^{n}

described above allow one to identify

\mathrm{Conf}_{n}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})=V\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\setminus\bigcup_{1\leq i<j\leq n}H_{ij}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathbb{R}}^{d},

that is, as the complement of subspace arrangements coming from the reflection hyperplane arrangements “thickened” by tensoring with
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
as in Theorems
9.1
,
9.2

and Remark
9.3
.
In fact, the special cases of those results for the braid arrangements, along with quadratic presentations for the associated algebras, were known to
Arnol’d
[
5
]
for
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
and
Cohen
[
27
]
for
\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\wedge_{\mathbb{k}}(x_{ij})/(x_{ij}x_{ik}-x_{ij}x_{jk}+x_{ik}x_{jk})

\displaystyle\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{k}[x_{ij}]/\,\,(x_{ij}x_{ik}-x_{ij}x_{jk}+x_{ik}x_{jk},\,\,x_{ij}^{2}\,\,)

Here permutations
\sigma
in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
act on the variables
by permuting subscripts, that is,
\sigma(x_{ij})=x_{\sigma(i),\sigma(j)}
, but with the convention that
x_{ji}=x_{ij}
in
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
, and
x_{ji}=-x_{ij}
in
\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
.

Note that that these presentations are consistent with the general presentation coming from supersolvable matroids in Corollary
5.14
, using the modular complete flag
\underline{F}
of flats chosen in
Example
4.7
: one checks that the corresponding decomposition
\underline{E}=(E_{1},E_{2},\ldots,E_{n-1})
of
E=\{\{i,j\}\}_{1\leq i<j\leq n}
has

(82)

\displaystyle E_{1}

\displaystyle=\{\{1,2\}\},

\displaystyle E_{2}

\displaystyle=\{\{1,3\},\{2,3\}\},

\displaystyle E_{3}

\displaystyle=\{\{1,4\},\{2,4\},\{3,4\}\},

\displaystyle\vdots

\displaystyle E_{n-1}

\displaystyle=\{\{1,n\},\{2,n\},\ldots,\{n-2,n\},\{n-1,n\}\},

and the subset of circuits

\mathcal{C}_{\operatorname{BEZ}}(\underline{E})=\{\{i,j\},\{i,k\},\{j,k\}\}_{1\leq i<j<k\leq n}.

Here the NBC monomial basis for either
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n}),\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
are the products of
x_{ij}
that
choose at most one
\{i,j\}
from each set
E_{p}
with
p=1,2,\ldots,n-1
above; Barcelo
[
11
, Thm. 2.1]
calls this
picking at most one finger
x_{ij}
from each hand
E_{p}
. Since the exponents
e_{p}=|E_{p}|
here are
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{r})=(1,2,\ldots,n-1)
,
one has these Hilbert series

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hilb}(\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n}),t)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hilb}(\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n}),t)

\displaystyle=(1+t)(1+2t)\cdots(1+(n-1)t)=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}t^{i}\,c(n,n-i)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hilb}(\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!},t)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hilb}(\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!},t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1-t)(1-2t)\cdots(1-(n-1)t)}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}t^{i}\,S((n-1)+i,n-1),

where the coefficients
c(n,k),S(n,k)
appearing here are the
(signless) Stirling numbers of the first kind

c(n,k)
, counting permutations in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
with
k
cycles, and the
Stirling numbers of the 2nd kind

S(n,k)
, counting partitions of the set
\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
into
k
blocks.
Comparing coefficients on powers of
t
, one has for either
A(n)=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
or
\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
that

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{k}}A(n)_{i}

\displaystyle=c(n,n-i),

\displaystyle c(n,k)

\displaystyle=\dim_{\mathbb{k}}A(n)_{n-k},

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{k}}A(n)^{!}_{i}

\displaystyle=S((n-1)+i,n-1),

\displaystyle S(n,k)

\displaystyle=\dim_{k}A(k+1)^{!}_{n-k}.

Definition 11.1
.

(
Stirling representations
)
For either
A(n)=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
or
A(n)=\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
,
call
A(n)_{i}
the
Stirling representations of the first kind
, and
call
A(n)^{!}_{i}
the
Stirling representations of the second kind
.
When emphasizing their dimensions as representations, we will abbreviate them as

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n,k)

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{n-k},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{\mathrm{VG}}(n,k)

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{n-k},\text{ both }\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{n}\text{-modules},

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n,k)

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{k+1})^{!}_{n-k},

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VG}}(n,k)

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{k+1})^{!}_{n-k},\text{ both }\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{k+1}\text{-modules}.

Remark 11.2
.

The coincidence between
\dim_{\mathbb{k}}A(k+1)^{!}_{n-k}
and
S(n,k)
, counting set partitions of
\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
into
k
blocks, is closely related to a well-known combinatorial encoding of set partitions via
restricted growth functions
, as we explain here;
see also Stanton and White
[
84
, Sec. 1.5]
.

Given any
k
-block set partition
\pi=\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{k}\}

of
\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
, re-index the blocks so
that
\min B_{1}<\min B_{2}<\cdots<\min B_{k}
.
Then the
restricted growth function (rgf)

encoding
\pi
is the sequence
(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})

defined by
i_{j}=\ell
if
j\in B_{\ell}
for
j=1,2,\ldots,n
. By definition,
i_{1}:=1
and
i_{j}\leq 1+\max\{i_{0},i_{1},\ldots,i_{j-1}\}
;
it is not hard to check that these two properties characterize the rgf’s. For example, with
n=15
and
k=3
, this set partition

\pi=\{\underbrace{\{\mathbf{1},2,3,5,8,10,15\}}_{B_{1}},\underbrace{\{\mathbf{4},6,7,12\}}_{B_{2}},\underbrace{\{\mathbf{9},11,13,14\}}_{B_{3}}\}

corresponds to this rgf
(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{15})
:

\begin{array}[]{rcccccccccccccccl}i_{1}&i_{2}&i_{3}&i_{4}&i_{5}&i_{6}&i_{7}&i_{8}&i_{9}&i_{10}&i_{11}&i_{12}&i_{13}&i_{14}&i_{15}\\
1&1&1&2&1&2&2&1&3&1&3&2&3&3&1\end{array}

We claim that these rgf’s correspond bijectively to the standard monomial
\mathbb{k}
-basis for
A(k+1)^{!}_{n-k}
given
in Corollary
5.18
. To explain
this bijection, underline the first (leftmost) occurrence of each value
p=1,2,\ldots,k
among the
i_{j}
, and append an extra (underlined)
i_{n+1}:=k+1
on the right, as a convention.
One then associates to
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})
the product

m_{2}\cdot m_{3}\cdots m_{k}\cdot m_{k+1}
where
m_{p}
is the noncommutative monomial in variables
\{y_{ip}\}_{i=1}^{p-1}
obtained by replacing each
non-underlined value
i_{j}
above
with the variable
x_{i_{j},p}
if
p
is the next underlined value to the right of
i_{j}
:

\begin{array}[]{rcccccccccccccccl}\underline{1}&1&1&\underline{2}&1&2&2&1&\underline{3}&1&3&2&3&3&1&\underline{4}\\
&y_{12}&y_{12}&\cdot&y_{13}&y_{23}&y_{23}&y_{13}&\cdot&y_{14}&y_{34}&y_{24}&y_{34}&y_{34}&y_{14}&\end{array}

Since the number of non-underlined values
i_{j}
is
n-k
,
this is a standard monomial in
A(k+1)^{!}_{n-k}
.

Remark 11.3
.

The presentations of
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!},\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}
in Theorem
5.18
are equivalent to what
Cohen and Gitler
[
26
]

called
graded infinitesimal braid relations
in their presentation of the loop space homology algebra
H_{*}(\Omega X,\mathbb{k})
where
X=\mathrm{Conf}_{n}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
; see also
Berglund
[
13
, Example 5.5]
.
For the case of
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}
, considered as a universal enveloping algebra
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}=\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{L})
, see also the discussion by Fresse
[
43
, Ch. 10]
referring to
\mathcal{L}
as the
Drinfeld-Kohno Lie algebra
and
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{L})
as the
algebra of chord diagrams
.

11.2.
Stirling representations of the first kind: generating functions

The
\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-module structure for either
A(n)=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
or
\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
are well-studied. Explicit irreducible decompositions are not known, but can be computed reasonably efficiently through symmetric function formulas involving plethysm and generating functions, given in work of
Sundaram and Welker
[
87
, Thm. 4.4(iii)]
and reviewed here; see also the summary in Hersh and Reiner
[
48
, Sec. 2]
.

Let
\mathbb{k}
be a field of characteristic zero.
The
Frobenius characteristic isomorphism

R_{\mathbb{k}}(\mathfrak{S}_{n})\cong\Lambda_{n}
, where
\Lambda_{n}
are the degree
n
homogeneous symmetric functions
\Lambda(z_{1},z_{2},\ldots)_{n}
, mentioned in Section
6.3
above, can be compiled for all
n
to give a ring isomorphism

\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}R_{\mathbb{k}}(\mathfrak{S}_{n})\overset{\mathrm{ch}}{\longrightarrow}\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\Lambda_{n}=\Lambda.

Here the product on the left is the
external
or
induction product

([U],[V])\longmapsto\left[(U\otimes_{\mathbb{k}}V)\uparrow_{\mathfrak{S}_{a}\times\mathfrak{S}_{b}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{a+b}}\right],

while the product on the right simply multiplies symmetric functions. If one defines the power sum symmetric function
p_{r}:=z_{1}^{r}+z_{2}^{r}+\cdots
, and
the
\mathbb{k}
-basis
\{p_{\lambda}:=p_{\lambda_{1}}p_{\lambda_{2}}\cdots\}
indexed by partitions
\lambda
of
n
for
\Lambda_{n}
, then for each
\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-module
U
with character
\chi_{U}
, the Frobenius isomorphism maps

[U]\overset{\mathrm{ch}}{\longmapsto}\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\chi_{U}(\sigma)\,p_{\lambda(\sigma)}

where
\lambda(\sigma)
is the cycle type partition of
\sigma
.

Let
\mathrm{Lie}_{n}
denote the
n^{th}

Lie representation
: the
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-representation
on the multilinear component of the free Lie algebra on
n
variables. It has a formula due to Klyachko
[
52
]
as

\mathrm{Lie}_{n}=\zeta\uparrow_{C_{n}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{n}},

where
\zeta
is the one-dimensional representation of the cyclic group
C_{n}

inside
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
generated by an
n
-cycle
c
,
that sends
c\mapsto e^{\frac{2\pi i}{n}}
.
Defining symmetric functions

\displaystyle\ell_{n}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{ch}(\mathrm{Lie}_{n}),

\displaystyle\pi_{n}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{ch}(\mathrm{sgn}_{n}\otimes\mathrm{Lie}_{n}),

and letting
(f,g)\mapsto f[g]
denote
plethystic composition
of symmetric functions
[
58
, §I.8]
, one has the following plethystic expressions and product generating functions
(see Sundaram
[
88
, Thm. 1.8, and p.249]
, Sundaram and Welker
[
87
, Thm. 4.4(iii)]
and Hersh and Reiner
[
48
, §2, Thm. 2.17]
):

(83)

\displaystyle 1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}u^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathrm{ch}([\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{n-k}])t^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda=1^{m_{1}}2^{m_{2}}\cdots}u^{|\lambda|}t^{\ell(\lambda)}\prod_{j\geq 1}h_{m_{j}}[\ell_{j}]

(84)

\displaystyle=\prod_{m=1}^{\infty}(1-u^{m}p_{m})^{-a_{m}(t)},

(85)

\displaystyle 1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}u^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathrm{ch}([\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{n-k}])t^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda=1^{m_{1}}2^{m_{2}}\cdots}u^{|\lambda|}t^{\ell(\lambda)}\prod_{j\text{odd }}h_{m_{j}}[\pi_{j}]\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\text{ even}\\
j\geq 2\end{subarray}}e_{m_{j}}[\pi_{j}],

(86)

\displaystyle=\prod_{m=1}^{\infty}(1+(-u)^{m}p_{m})^{a_{m}(-t)},

where here

a_{m}(t)\coloneqq\frac{1}{m}\sum_{d|m}\mu(d)t^{\frac{m}{d}},

with
\mu(d)
the number-theoretic Möbius function.
Equivalently, define for a partition
\lambda=1^{m_{1}}2^{m_{2}}\cdots
of
n
(written
\lambda\vdash n
) with
m_{i}
parts equal to
i
, the
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-representations
\mathrm{OS}_{\lambda},\mathrm{VG}_{\lambda}
whose Frobenius characteristics are
the products appearing above. Then

(87)

\begin{array}[]{rclcccl}\mathrm{ch}\,\mathrm{VG}_{\lambda}&:=&\displaystyle\prod_{i}h_{m_{i}}[\ell_{i}],&\text{ so that }&\mathrm{ch}(\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{n-k})&=&\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n\\
\ell(\lambda)=k\end{subarray}}\mathrm{VG}_{\lambda},\\
\mathrm{ch}\,\mathrm{OS}_{\lambda}&:=&\displaystyle\prod_{i\ \mathrm{odd}}h_{m_{i}}[\pi_{i}]\prod_{i\ \mathrm{even}}e_{m_{i}}[\pi_{i}],&\text{ so that }&\mathrm{ch}(\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{n-k})&=&\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\vdash n\\
\ell(\lambda)=k\end{subarray}}\mathrm{OS}_{\lambda}.\\
\end{array}

Thus
\mathrm{VG}_{(n)}
is the
Lie representation
with
\mathrm{ch}\mathrm{VG}_{(n)}=\ell_{n}
mentioned above.
Similarly,
\{\mathrm{VG}_{\lambda}\}
are called
higher Lie characters
; see
Schocker
[
76
]
.
Also, the last equality in (
87
) implies that
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{n-k}
coincides with the
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-representation on the
(n-k)
th
Whitney homology
of the partition lattice,
1\leq k\leq n
; see Lehrer and Solomon
[
55
, Thm. 4.5]
, Sundaram
[
88
, Thm. 1.8]
.

11.3.
Data on Stirling representations of the second kind

In contrast to the above
\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-descriptions of

A(n)_{i}
when
A(n)=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n}),\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
, for the Koszul duals
A(n)^{!}_{i}
, we currently lack formulas of this nature, although we can
tabulate
A(n)^{!}_{i}
recursively from the
A(n)_{i}
using (
17
).

Question 11.4
.

Are there formulas like (
83
), (
84
), (
85
), (
86
)
for the duals
\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!},\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}
?

11.4.
Branching rules for both kinds of Stirling representations

Stirling numbers of both kinds satisfy well-known recurrences, mentioned in the Introduction:

(88)

\displaystyle c(n,k)

\displaystyle=(n-1)\cdot c(n-1,k)+c(n-1,k-1)

(89)

\displaystyle S(n,k)

\displaystyle=k\cdot S(n-1,k)+S(n-1,k-1)

Theorem
7.1
will allow us to lift these to branching rules for the Stirling representations of both kinds. We consider here the action of
G=\mathfrak{S}_{n}
on the matroid and oriented matroid
\operatorname{Br}_{n}
.
In this case, the setwise
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-stabilizer for the modular coatom

F=E_{n-2}
in (
82
)
is the subgroup
H=\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}
.
Furthermore, the permutation action
\mathcal{X}
of
\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}
on the set

E_{n-1}=E\setminus F=\{\{1,n\},\{2,n\},\ldots,\{n-1,n\}\}

and its signed permtuation action on the real vectors representing
E_{n-1}

in the oriented matroid
\operatorname{Br}_{n}

\{v_{1n},v_{2n},\ldots,v_{n-1,n}\}=\{e_{1}-e_{n},e_{2}-e_{n},\cdots,e_{n-1}-e_{n}\},

are both equivalent to the defining

\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}
-permutation representation
\chi^{(n-1)}_{\mathrm{def}}

via
(n-1)\times(n-1)
permutation matrices.
Translating Theorem
7.1
then immediately
implies the following.

Corollary 11.5
.

For any field
\mathbb{k}
, the recurrences (
88
), (
89
) lift equivariantly as follows.

(i)

Letting
\mathfrak{c}(n,k)
denote either
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{n-k}
or
\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{n-k}
as
\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-module, the recurrence (
88
) lifts to two recurrences in
R_{\mathbb{k}}(\mathfrak{S}_{n-1})

(90)

[\mathfrak{c}(n,k)\downarrow^{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}_{\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}}]=[\chi^{(n-1)}_{\mathrm{def}}]\cdot[\mathfrak{c}(n-1,k)]+[\mathfrak{c}(n-1,k-1)],

reflecting two
\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}
-module exact sequences

(91)

0\longrightarrow\mathfrak{c}(n-1,k-1)\longrightarrow\mathfrak{c}(n,k)\downarrow^{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}_{\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}}\longrightarrow\chi^{(n-1)}_{\mathrm{def}}\otimes\mathfrak{c}(n-1,k)\longrightarrow 0.

(ii)

Letting
\mathcal{S}(n,k)
denote either
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{k+1})^{!}_{n-k}
or
\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{k+1})^{!}_{n-k}
as
\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{k+1}
-module, the recurrence (
89
) lifts to two relations in
R_{\mathbb{k}}(\mathfrak{S}_{k})

(92)

[\mathcal{S}(n,k)\downarrow^{\mathfrak{S}_{k+1}}_{\mathfrak{S}_{k}}]=[\chi^{(k)}_{\mathrm{def}}]\cdot[\mathcal{S}(n-1,k)\downarrow^{\mathfrak{S}_{k+1}}_{\mathfrak{S}_{k}}]+[\mathcal{S}(n-1,k-1)],

reflecting two
\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{k}
-module exact sequences

(93)

0\longrightarrow\chi^{(k)}_{\mathrm{def}}\otimes\mathcal{S}(n-1,k)\downarrow^{\mathfrak{S}_{k+1}}_{\mathfrak{S}_{k}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}(n,k)\downarrow^{\mathfrak{S}_{k+1}}_{\mathfrak{S}_{k}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}(n-1,k-1)\longrightarrow 0.

Remark 11.6
.

Proposition
2.16
implies the two versions of (
90
) are equivalent to those of (
92
).

Remark 11.7
.

All of the assertions Corollary
11.5
are new, as far as we know, when working over
fields
\mathbb{k}
of positive characteristic, and (
91
),(
92
),(
93
) are new even when
\mathbb{k}
has characteristic zero.
However, when
\mathbb{k}
has characteristic zero, it turns out that (
90
) also follows from work of Sundaram in
[
88
,
89
]
.
For example, the relation (
90
) for
\mathfrak{c}(n,k)=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{n-k}
can be deduced by combining
[
88
, Thm 2.2, Part (2) and Prop. 1.9]
; we omit the details here.
Also it turns out that both cases of (
90
), when either
\mathfrak{c}(n,k)=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{n-k}
or
\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{n-k}
, follow from the symmetric function branching result
[
89
, Thm. 4.10]
. In the notation there, choosing
F=\sum_{n\geq 1}\ell_{n}
, one takes
G^{j}_{n}=h_{j}[F]|_{\deg n}
to deduce
(
90
) for

\mathfrak{c}(n,k)=\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{n-k}
,
and one takes
G^{j}_{n}=e_{j}[F]|_{\deg n}

to deduce (
90
) for

\mathfrak{c}(n,k)=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{n-k}
. We
again omit the details here.

11.5.
Braid matroids and representation stability

Here we wish to apply the representation stability
results of Section
10
to the
braid matroids
\operatorname{Br}_{n}
. A special case of the main result of Church and Farb
[
25
]
shows, in our language, that for each fixed
i=0,1,2\ldots
, both sequences
\{A(n)_{i}\}
where

A(n)=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n}),\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
are representation stable. Hersh and Reiner
[
48
, Thm. 1.1]
pinned down the onset of this representation stability.

Theorem 11.8
.

For each fixed
i\geq 1
,
both sequences
\{A(n)_{i}\}
where

A(n)=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
and
\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
are representation stable, past
3i
for
\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
and past
3i+1
for
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
.

One then deduces the following representation stability for their
Koszul duals.

Corollary 11.9
.

For each fixed
i\geq 1
,
both sequences
\{A(n)^{!}_{i}\}
where

A(n)=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
and
\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
are representation stable, past
3i
for
\{\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{i}\}
and past
4i
for
\{\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{i}\}
.

Proof.

Theorem
11.8
gives the necessary hypotheses to apply Corollary
10.6
, using the constant
c=3
for
\{\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{i}\}
and using the constant
c=4
(since
3i+1\leq 4i
)
for
\{\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{i}\}
.
∎

Remark 11.10
.

The bounds in Corollary
11.9
happen to be tight for
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{i},\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{i}
when
i=0,1,2
. To see this, one can apply Lemma
10.5
to
Propositions
B.1
,
B.2
and Remark
B.3
below (specifically, see equations (
106
),
(
108
)) to deduce that
for
i=0,1,2
, the sequences
\{\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{i}^{!}\}
start to stabilize exactly when
n\geq 4i
, and the sequences
\{\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{i}^{!}\}
start to stabilize exactly when
n\geq 3i
. This suggests
the following conjecture, confirmed by
Sage/Cocalc
for
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{i}^{!}
up to
i=5
, and for
\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{i}^{!}
up to
i=7
.

Conjecture 11.11
.

The bounds for onset of stability in Corollary
11.9
are tight:
for
i\geq 0
, the sequences
\{\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{i}^{!}\}
and
\{\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{i}^{!}\}

start to stabilize exactly when
n=4i
and
n=3i
, respectively.

Since
A(n)=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n}),\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})

are anti-commutative and commutative, respectively,
Corollary
10.10
immediately implies the following.

Corollary 11.12
.

Letting
A(n)=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n}),\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
, and defining

\mathcal{L}(n)_{i}
by
A(n)^{!}=\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{L}(n))
, for each fixed
i=1,2,\ldots
, the sequence
\{\mathcal{L}(n)_{i}\}
is
representation stable.

Remark 11.13
.

The case of Corollary
11.12
for
A(n)=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
also follows from work of Church, Ellenberg and Farb
[
24
, Thm. 7.3.4]
. They consider instead
of
\mathcal{L}(n)
the
Malcev Lie algebra

\mathfrak{p}_{n}
associated to the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X)
for the configuration space
X=\mathrm{Conf}_{n}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})=\mathrm{Conf}_{n}({\mathbb{C}})
considered in Section
11.1
; alternatively,
X
is the complement of the complex braid arrangement
\mathcal{A}
as in Theorem
9.1
. These two Lie algebras

\mathfrak{p}_{n}
and
\mathcal{L}(n)

coincide due to the
1
-formality of complements of complex algebraic hypersurfaces; see, e.g., Suciu and Wang
[
86
, §6,7]
.

Computations in
Sage/Cocalc
through
i=8
suggest the following conjecture.

Conjecture 11.14
.

Defining
\{\mathcal{L}(n)_{i}\}
by
A(n)^{!}=\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{L}(n))
, its onset of representation stability is:

•

n=2i
for a fixed
i\geq 1
when
A(n)=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
,

•

n=2i
for a fixed
i\geq 3
when
A(n)=\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
.

11.6.
Near-boundary cases for
Stirling representations of the second kind

Stirling numbers
S(n,k)
of the second
kind have more explicit formulas
when either
k
or
n-k
is small.
We similarly present here more explicit formulas,
in the language of symmetric functions
for the Stirling representations

\displaystyle\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}((n-1)+i,n-1),

\displaystyle\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VG}}((n-1)+i,n-1),

as
\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-modules, when either
i
or
n
is small.

Part of our motivation comes from the following
observations about when
\mathrm{OS}(M)_{i},\mathrm{VG}(\mathcal{M})_{i}

and their Koszul duals
\mathrm{OS}(M)^{!}_{i},\mathrm{VG}(\mathcal{M})^{!}_{i}
turn out to be
permutation representations

of their automorphism groups
G=\text{Aut}(M)
or
\text{Aut}(\mathcal{M})
. The discussion of Boolean matroids Section
6.1
and low rank matroids
in Section
6.2
and Proposition
6.1

showed that

•

\mathrm{OS}(M)_{i},\mathrm{VG}(\mathcal{M})_{i}
are
rarely
permutation representations,

•

\mathrm{VG}(\mathcal{M})^{!}_{i}
is
not always
a permutation representation,

•

but
\mathrm{OS}(M)^{!}_{i}
was
always
a
G
-permutation representation in
these previous examples.

For the braid matroids
\operatorname{Br}_{n}
, it is

not always
true that
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{i}
is
a permutation representation, but the next result shows that it happens
in many cases where
i
or
n
is small.

Theorem 11.15
.

For
\mathbb{k}
of characteristic zero,
the

\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-modules

\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{i}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}((n-1)+i,n-1)

(i)

are permutation modules for
i=0,1
,

(ii)

are
half
-permutation modules for
i=2
, meaning that

2\cdot[\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{2}]
is the class of a permutation module in
R_{\mathbb{k}}(\mathfrak{S}_{n})
,

(iii)

are permutation modules
9
9

9

And

[\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{i}]
are even
h
-positive
permutation modules when
n=1,2,3
, overlapping with the discussion
in Section
6.3
on rank two matroids, as
U_{2,3}=\operatorname{Br}_{3}
.
for
n=1,2,3,4,5
.

However, both

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(10,5)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{6})^{!}_{5},

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(11,6)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{7})^{!}_{5}

fail to be permutation modules, even after scaling them by positive integers, since they can be shown to have negative character values.

10
10

10

Trevor Karn’s Burnside Solver further shows that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(5+i,5)
is a permutation module for
i\leq 4
and
i=6,7,10
; it fails to be one at
i=8,9
.

i
n

3
4

Permutation module
Not
a “fraction” of a permutation module
Half-permutation module

Table 1
.
When are
[\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{i}]=\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}((n-1)+i,n-1)
permutation modules or “fractions” thereof?

summarizes the results of

11.15
; an outline of the proof appears in

.

12.
Further remarks and questions

We remark here on some further directions which
could merit exploration.

12.1.
Projective geometries over finite fields

The Boolean matroids
U_{n,n}
discussed in Example
4.5
and Section
6.1

have a well-studied “
q
-analogue”: the
projective geometries

PG(n,q)
, associated with the finite vector spaces
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
. These
PG(n,q)
are non-orientable simple matroids whose ground set
E={\mathbb{P}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n})={\mathbb{P}}^{n-1}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}
is the set of points in a finite projective space, so
|E|=[n]_{q}:=1+q+q^{2}+\cdots+q^{n-1}
, with poset of flats
\mathcal{F}
given by the lattice of all subspaces in
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
; see Oxley
[
69
, §6.1]
and Orlik and Terao
[
68
, Example 4.33]
. The lattices
\mathcal{F}
are
modular
, meaning that every flat is a modular element, so that every complete flag
\underline{F}
is a modular complete flag.
Hence the matroids
PG(n,q)
are supersolvable, with exponents
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})=(1,q,q^{2},\ldots,q^{n-1})
.
Consequently, the family of
\mathbb{k}
-algebras
A(n):=\mathrm{OS}(PG(n,q))
is Koszul,
satisfying

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hilb}(A(n),t)

\displaystyle=(1+t)(1+qt)(1+q^{2}t)\cdots(1+q^{n-1}t)\quad\text{ with }\dim_{\mathbb{k}}A(n)^{!}_{i}=q^{\binom{i}{2}}\left[\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{i}\right]_{q}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hilb}(A(n)^{!},t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1-t)(1-qt)(1-q^{2}t)\cdots(1-q^{n-1}t)}\quad\text{ with }\dim_{\mathbb{k}}A(n)^{!}_{i}=\left[\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n+i-1}{i}\right]_{q},

where
\left[\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}\right]_{q}:=\frac{[n]!_{q}}{[k]!_{q}[n-k]!_{q}}

with
[n]!_{q}:=[n]_{q}[n-1]_{q}\cdots[2]_{q}[1]_{q}
; see Macdonald
[
58
, Example I.2.2]
.

Problem 12.1
.

Study
A(n)=\mathrm{OS}(PG(n,q))
and
A(n)^{!}=\mathrm{OS}(PG(n,q))^{!}
as
GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
-representations.

For example, the
q
-Pascal recurrences
for
A(n)_{i}=\left[\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{i}\right]_{q}
and
A(n)^{!}_{i}=\left[\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n+i-1}{i}\right]_{q}
will have lifts to branching rules via Proposition
2.16
and Theorem
7.1
. There is also an appropriate
analogue here of
representation stability for
GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
-representations
developed by Putman and Sam
[
73
]
.

12.2.
Type
B
, wreath products, and Dowling geometries

As mentioned in Section
11
, the braid matroids
\operatorname{Br}_{n}
are represented by the
root systems of type
A_{n-1}
, accounting for the action of the reflection group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
on them as symmetries.

There are other real and complex reflection groups giving rise to matroids with large symmetry, but relatively few of these matroids are supersolvable; see Hoge and Röhrle
[
49
]
for their classification. They include the dihedral reflection groups giving rise to the
rank two matroids already discussed in Example
4.6
and Section
6.3
.
They also include the
reflection groups of type
B_{n}
or
C_{n}
, isomorphic to the
hyperoctahedral group
or
signed permutation group

\mathfrak{S}^{\pm}_{n}
that appeared in Section
5.2
. Their root systems can be realized over
{\mathbb{R}}
, giving rise to an oriented matroid
from the positive roots

(94)

\Phi_{B_{n}}^{+}:=\{+e_{i}\pm e_{j}\}_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\,\,\sqcup\,\,\{e_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}.

More generally, one has the complex reflection groups
\mathfrak{S}_{n}[{\mathbb{Z}}/m{\mathbb{Z}}]=({\mathbb{Z}}/m{\mathbb{Z}})\wr\mathfrak{S}_{n}
for
m\geq 2
, also known as the groups
G(m,1,n)
within Shephard and Todd’s classification
[
78
]

of irreducible complex reflection groups. Letting
\zeta_{m}:=e^{\frac{2\pi i}{m}}
, their associated matroids can be represented by this list of vectors in
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
:

(95)

\{e_{i}-\zeta^{k}e_{j}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i<j\leq n\\
0\leq k\leq m-1\end{subarray}}\,\,\sqcup\,\,\{e_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}.

These matroids are not realizable over
{\mathbb{R}}
(and not orientable) unless
m=2
where they recover the type
B_{n}/C_{n}
reflection groups.

Motivated by these examples,
Dowling
[
36
]
introduced a more general class of matroids, now known as
the
Dowling geometries

{Q_{n}(G)}
; see Oxley
[
69
, §6.10]
for definitions and discussion.
Here
G
is
any
finite group, and the matroid automorphisms of
{Q_{n}(G)}

contain the wreath product

\mathfrak{S}_{n}[G]=G\wr\mathfrak{S}_{n}
.
Interestingly, Dowling proved that the matroid
{Q_{n}(G)}
is representable over a field
{\mathbb{F}}

if and only if
the finite group
G
is a subgroup of
{\mathbb{F}}^{\times}
; in particular, this forces
G
to be cyclic,
as in the complex reflection
groups
\mathfrak{S}_{n}[{\mathbb{Z}}/m{\mathbb{Z}}]
mentioned above.

Dowling also showed that
{Q_{n}(G)}

is supersolvable for any finite group
G
. Consequently,
their Orlik-Solomon algebras
\mathrm{OS}({Q_{n}(G)})
are always Koszul, and when
|G|=2
, the same holds for the Varchenko-Gel’fand ring
\mathrm{VG}(\mathcal{M}(B_{n}))
, e.g., if
\mathcal{M}(B_{n})
is realized by the vectors in (
94
) above.

Problem 12.2
.

Study these families of Koszul algebras
A(n)=\mathrm{OS}({Q_{n}(G)})
and
\mathrm{VG}(\mathcal{M}(B_{n}))
, along with their Koszul duals
A(n)^{!}
,
as
\mathfrak{S}_{n}[G]
-representations.

If
m:=|G|
, then the exponents for the supersolvable matroids
{Q_{n}(G)}
turn out to be

(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{n})=(1,m+1,2m+1,\ldots,(n-1)m+1).

Combining this with Dowling’s formulas
[
36
, §4]
, for the rank sizes
11
11

11

Also called the
Whitney numbers of the second kind for the poset.
in the poset of flats of

{Q_{n}(G)}
,
one encounters a similar
coincidence to the equality

\dim_{\mathbb{k}}\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{i}=S((n-1)+i,n-1)
discussed in Remark
11.2
: the dimension of
\mathrm{OS}({Q_{n}(G)})^{!}_{i}
is
the size of the
(n-1)^{st}
rank in the flat poset of
{Q_{(n-1)+i}(G)}
.
This again reflects a bijection
between the standard monomial
\mathbb{k}
-basis for

\mathrm{OS}({Q_{n}(G)})^{!}_{i}
from Theorem
5.18

and an encoding of flats in
{Q_{n}(G)}
generalizing restricted growth
functions, similar to work of
Komatsu, Bagno, and Garber
[
8
, §2.3]
.
We omit the details here.

12.3.
Equivariant degree one injections

Recall the following consequences of Theorem
5.21
: By Part (ii) of Corollary
5.22
,
for the
matroid
automorphism group
G=\text{Aut}(M)
, there are
G
-equivariant degree one injections

(96)

[\mathrm{OS}(M)^{!}_{i}]\hookrightarrow[\mathrm{OS}(M)^{!}_{i+1}],\text{ for all }i\geq 0

while Part (iii) of Corollary
5.22
asserts that
for the full
oriented
matroid automorphism group
G=\text{Aut}(\mathcal{M})
, there are
G
-equivariant degree
two
injections

[\mathrm{VG}(\mathcal{M})^{!}_{i}]\hookrightarrow[\mathrm{VG}(\mathcal{M})_{i+2}^{!}],\text{ for all }\ i\geq 0.

The latter injections arise from right-multiplication by a degree two
G
-invariant
\underline{E}
-generic power sum
p_{2}(\mathbf{y})
, such as
p_{2}(\mathbf{y})=\sum_{i}y_{i}^{2}
.
Unfortunately, for some oriented matroids
\mathcal{M}
, there are no degree one
\underline{E}
-generic element power sums
p_{1}(\mathbf{y})
in
A^{!}_{1}
that are also
G
-invariant. For example,
p_{1}(\mathbf{y})=\sum_{i}y_{i}
is
not
always
G
-invariant.
In fact, the calculation for rank one oriented matroids
\mathcal{M}=U_{1,1}
in (
55
)
shows that in that case,
there are no

G
-equivariant injections
\mathrm{VG}(\mathcal{M})^{!}_{i}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{VG}(\mathcal{M})_{i+1}^{!}
for any
i
.

Nonetheless, for the braid matroids
\mathcal{M}=\operatorname{Br}_{n}
,
Sage
calculations for
n\leq 10
and
1\leq i\leq 9
support the following conjecture.

Conjecture 12.3
.

For the braid oriented matroid
\mathcal{M}=\operatorname{Br}_{n}
,
there exist equivariant injections

\displaystyle[\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VG}}((n-1)+i,n-1)]=[{\mathrm{VG}}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{i}]

\displaystyle\quad\hookrightarrow[\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VG}}((n-1)+i+1,n-1)]=[\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{i+1}],\text{ for all }i\geq 1.

Propositions
B.4
and
B.8
establish Conjecture
12.3
for
n\leq 4
in characteristic zero.

We close with some observations on
a consequence of the
G
-equivariant injections
in (
96
):
they imply that the following alternating sum in
R_{\mathbb{k}}(G)
is always the class of a genuine
\mathbb{k}G
-module:

(97)

[\mathrm{OS}^{!}(M)_{i}]-[\mathrm{OS}^{!}(M)_{i-1}]+\ldots+(-1)^{i-1}[\mathrm{OS}^{!}(M)_{0}]

Problem 12.4
.

Investigate the genuine
\mathbb{k}G
-modules (
97
).
Do they have interesting descriptions?

For example, for the braid matroid
M=\operatorname{Br}_{n}
,
the dimension of the genuine module (
97
) is

(98)

S(n\!-\!1\!+\!i,n\!-\!1)-S(n\!-\!2\!+\!i,n\!-\!1)+\cdots+(-1)^{i}S(n\!-\!1\!,n\!-\!1).

This has an interpretation via a result of Mansour and Munagi
[
59
, Corollary 11]
: it is the number of set partitions of
\{1,2,\ldots,n+i\}
into
n
blocks, where no block contains a pair
j,j+1
modulo
n+i
for
1\leq j\leq n+i
. We know of
no accompanying
\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-module
built from these objects.

We remark that for any matroid
M
, the alternating sum analogous to (
97
) for
\mathrm{OS}(M)
, namely

(99)

[\mathrm{OS}(M)_{i}]-[\mathrm{OS}(M)_{i-1}]+\ldots+(-1)^{i-1}[\mathrm{OS}(M)_{0}]

is always a genuine
\mathbb{k}G
-module for
G=\text{Aut}(M)
, isomorphic to the top homology of a rank-selected subposet of the lattice of flats. We quickly sketch how this follows from combining these two results:

•

[
67
]
exhibits a
\mathbb{k}G
-module isomorphism
\mathrm{OS}(M)\cong\text{\bf Whit}(\mathcal{L}_{M})
, where
\text{\bf Whit}(\mathcal{L}_{M})
is the Whitney homology of the lattice of flats
\mathcal{L}_{M}
of
M
, and

•

[
88
]
If
G=\text{Aut}(P)
for a Cohen-Macaulay poset
P
, then the alternating sum in
{\mathbb{R}}_{\mathbb{k}}(G)

[\text{\bf Whit}_{i}(P)]-[\text{\bf Whit}_{i-1}(P)]+\ldots+(-1)^{i-1}[\text{\bf Whit}_{0}(P)]

is
\mathbb{k}G
-isomorphic to the top homology of the rank-selected subposet of
P
consisting of the bottom
i
nonzero ranks. The Hopf trace argument in
[
88
, Lemma 1.1]
, written for characteristic zero, can be replaced by applying, for an arbitrary field
\mathbb{k}
,
Proposition
2.11
(ii) to Baclawski’s complex. Similarly the arguments of Baclawski and Björner as cited in
[
88
, Theorem 1.2]
can be adapted for any field
\mathbb{k}
, by appealing to the isomorphism in

[
16
, p. 262, Theorem 7.9.6]
. Finally, the equivariant isomorphism with
\mathrm{OS}(M)
follows from

[
16
, Theorem 7.10.2]
, extending the argument of
[
67
, Theorem 4.3]
to the whole Orlik-Solomon algebra.

Appendix B
Proof of Theorem
11.15

The proof of Theorem
11.15

and calculation of near-boundary cases for

\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{i},\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{i}

employ a brute-force strategy, which we outline here, giving only
brief sketches of the arguments.

Note that since all
\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-modules
U

are self-contragredient, one has

[U^{*}]=[U]
in
R_{\mathbb{k}}(\mathfrak{S}_{n})
, and
so the defining recurrence (
17
) for Koszul modules simplifies to this:

(100)

[A^{!}_{d}]=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(-1)^{i-1}[A_{i}]\cdot[(A^{!}_{d-i})]

This means that if one defines

\displaystyle g_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ch}\,\mathrm{OS}(n)_{i}\text{ for }0\leq i\leq n-1,

\displaystyle f_{i}

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{ch}\,\mathrm{OS}(n)^{!}_{i}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n-1+i,n-1)\text{ for }i=0,1,2,\ldots,

then, with
*
below denoting the internal (Kronecker) product of symmetric functions, (
87
) lets one sometimes compute explicit formulas for the
g_{i}
in terms of the homogeneous symmetric functions
\{h_{\lambda}\}
, and (
100
) gives a recurrence for
f_{i}
in terms of
f_{0},f_{1},\ldots,f_{i-1}
:

(101)

f_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}(-1)^{i-1}g_{i}*f_{d-i}

In each of the cases below, we identify a small subset
T
of partitions of
n
such that the linear span of
\{h_{\lambda}:\lambda\in T\}
contains the
f_{i}
. Further manipulation then gives the results described in
Theorem

11.15
, and the precise formulas below.

B.1.
Proof of Theorem
11.15
part (i).

Corresponding to the Stirling number formulas

\displaystyle S(n-1,n-1)

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle S(n,n-1)

\displaystyle=\binom{n}{2},

one has the following result, implying Theorem
11.15
part (i).

Proposition B.1
.

For the cases
i=0,1
, one has

(102)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n-1,n-1)=\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VG}}(n-1,n-1)

\displaystyle=h_{n}\quad\text{ for }n\geq 1,

(103)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n,n-1)

\displaystyle=h_{2}h_{n-2}\quad\text{ for }n\geq 2,

(104)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VG}}(n,n-1)

\displaystyle=e_{2}h_{n-2}\quad\text{ for }n\geq 2.

Proof.

Equation (
102
) follows since
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{0}=\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{0}=\mathbb{k}
, carrying the trivial
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
representation in either case.
For (
103
), (
104
), note that
(
100
) and (
87
) imply

\displaystyle[\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{1}]

\displaystyle=[\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{1}]=[\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{(21^{n-2})}]=h_{1}[\pi_{2}]\cdot h_{n-2}[\pi_{1}]=h_{2}h_{n-2},,

\displaystyle[\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{1}]

\displaystyle=[\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{1}]=[\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})_{(21^{n-2})}]=h_{1}[\ell_{2}]\cdot h_{n-2}[\ell_{1}]=e_{2}h_{n-2}.\qed

B.2.
Proof of Theorem
11.15
part (ii).

Here we prove the curious fact that for
n\geq 7
,
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{2}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n+1,n-1)
is in fact
half
of a permutation module.

For
n=7,8,9,10
,
Sage
computation with the Burnside ring shows that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n+1,n-1)
is NOT a permutation module. By running his Burnside solver on the first formula in Proposition
B.2
below with rational coefficients, Trevor Karn noticed positive half-integers in the data and conjectured that two copies of

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n+1,n-1)
together constitute a permutation module.

Proposition B.2
.

One has the following decompositions as permutation modules for
n\leq 6
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(3,1)

\displaystyle=h_{2},

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(4,2)

\displaystyle=h_{3}+h_{1}^{3},

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(5,3)

\displaystyle=h_{2}[h_{1}^{2}]+h_{1}^{2}h_{2}+h_{4},

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(6,4)

\displaystyle=h_{1}\cdot h_{2}[h_{2}]+h_{2}(h_{3}+e_{3})+h_{2}^{2}h_{1},

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(7,5)

\displaystyle=h_{2}[h_{2}h_{1}]+h_{3}^{2}+h_{4}h_{1}^{2},

and then for
n\geq 4
one has

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}\,\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n+1,n-1)

(105)

\displaystyle=h_{n-2}h_{2}+h_{n-3}h_{1}^{3}+h_{n-3}h_{3}+h_{n-4}h_{2}^{2}+h_{n-4}h_{4}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{-h_{n-3}h_{2}h_{1}-h_{n-4}h_{3}h_{1}}}

(106)

\displaystyle=h_{n-2}\,s_{(2)}+h_{n-3}\,(s_{(1^{3})}+s_{(2,1)}+s_{(3)})+h_{n-4}\,(s_{(2,2)}+s_{(4)})

(107)

\displaystyle=h_{n-2}h_{2}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{1}{2}}h_{n-3}h_{1}^{3}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\frac{1}{2}}h_{n-3}(h_{3}+e_{3})+h_{n-4}\cdot h_{2}[h_{2}].

So two copies of

\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{2}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n+1,n-1)
together form a permutation module, with orbit stabilisers

\{\mathfrak{S}_{n-2}\times S_{2},\,\,\mathfrak{S}_{n-3},\,\,\mathfrak{S}_{n-3}\times C_{3},\,\,\mathfrak{S}_{n-4}\times I_{2}(4)\}

where
C_{n}
is the cyclic group of order
n
(generated by the
n
-cycle
(1,2,\ldots,n)
in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
) and
I_{2}(n)
is the dihedral group of order
2n
inside
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
containing that same
n
-cycle.

Sketch of proof.

The expansion for
n=2
is clear. For
n\geq 3
, writing
f_{i},g_{i}
as in (
101
), one finds that

\displaystyle f_{1}

\displaystyle=g_{1},

\displaystyle f_{2}

\displaystyle=f_{1}*g_{1}-g_{2}.

Using (
87
) and writing
\delta(S)\in\{0,1\}
depending on whether statement
S
is false or true, one has

\displaystyle f_{1}=g_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ch}\,A(n)_{1}=\mathrm{ch}\,\mathrm{OS}_{(2,1^{n-2})}=h_{n-2}\pi_{2}=h_{n-2}h_{2},

\displaystyle g_{2}

\displaystyle=h_{n-3}(h_{2}h_{1}-h_{3})\cdot\delta_{n\geq 3}+h_{n-4}(h_{3}h_{1}-h_{4})\cdot\delta_{n\geq 4}.

Using the standard fact that
U\otimes(V\big\uparrow_{H}^{G})\,\cong(U\big\downarrow_{H}\otimes V)\big\uparrow_{H}^{G},
for the Young subgroup
H=\mathfrak{S}_{2}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n-2},
and the
skewing operators

s_{(2)}^{\perp}
and
s_{(1^{2})}^{\perp}
as defined in Macdonald
[
58
, Ex. I.5.3]
, the expression (
105
) follows by routine manipulation. Then to establish (
107
), we
use these facts:

\displaystyle h_{1}^{3}+2h_{3}-2h_{2}h_{1}=h_{3}+e_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ch}\,1\big\uparrow_{C_{3}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{3}},

\displaystyle h_{2}[h_{2}]=h_{4}+s_{(2,2)}=h_{4}+h_{2}^{2}-h_{3}h_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ch}\,1\big\uparrow_{I_{2}(4)}^{\mathfrak{S}_{4}}.\qed

Remark B.3
.

A similar analysis gives the following for
\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n}^{!})_{2}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VG}}(n+1,n-1)

\displaystyle=h_{n-2}h_{2}+h_{n-3}(h_{3}-h_{2}h_{1}+h_{1}^{3})+h_{n-4}(h_{4}+h_{2}h_{1}^{2}-h_{3}h_{1}-h_{2}^{2}),\,n\geq 4

(108)

\displaystyle=h_{n-2}\,s_{(2)}+h_{n-3}\,(s_{(1^{3})}+s_{(2,1)}+s_{(3)})+h_{n-4}\,s_{(2,1,1)}.

Also
\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VG}}(n+1,n-1)=h_{1}^{n}+h_{n}
for
n=3,4,
and hence

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VG}}(n+1,n-1)
is a permutation module for
n\leq 4
; it is half a permutation module for
n=5
. However for
n=6,7,
the Burnside solver shows that it is not a permutation module (even after scaling), even though all character values are nonnegative.
At
n=8
there are negative character values, so even scaling will not result in a permutation module.

B.3.
Proof of Theorem
11.15
part (iii).

For fixed small
k
, the general Stirling number formula

(109)

S(n,k)={\frac{1}{k!}}\sum_{i=0}^{k}(-1)^{i}{\binom{k}{i}}(k-i)^{n}

gives fairly simple explicit formulas for
S(n,k)
as a function of
n
, e.g., for
k=1,2,3,4,5
:

(110)

\displaystyle S(n,1)

\displaystyle=1

(111)

\displaystyle S(n,2)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(2^{n}-2\cdot 1^{n})=2^{n-1}-1=1+2+2^{2}+\cdots+2^{n-2},

(112)

\displaystyle S(n,3)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}(3^{n}-3\cdot 2^{n}+3)

(113)

\displaystyle S(n,4)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{24}(4^{n}-4\cdot 3^{n}+6\cdot 2^{n}-4)

(114)

\displaystyle S(n,5)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{120}(5^{n}-5\cdot 4^{n}+10\cdot 3^{n}-10\cdot 2^{n}+5)

We give here analogous descriptions of the
\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-modules
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{i},\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{i}
, having dimension
S(n-1+i,n-1)
,
starting
12
12

12

There is little to say for
n=1
, as
\mathfrak{S}_{1}
is the trivial group, and

\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{1})=\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{1})=\mathbb{k}=\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{1})^{!}=\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{1})^{!},

and

\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(0,0)=\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VG}}(0,0)=h_{1}.
with
n=2,3
.

Proposition B.4
.

The Frobenius characteristics of the
\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-modules

\displaystyle\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n-1+i,n-1),

\displaystyle\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VG}}(n-1+i,n-1)

for
n=2,3
have these formulas:

n=2:

\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(i+1,1)=h_{2},

\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VG}}(i+1,1)=\begin{cases}h_{2},&i\text{ even},\\
e_{2},&i\text{ odd}.\end{cases}

n=3:

\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{OS}(i+2,2)=\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{VG}(i+2,2)=\frac{2^{i}-1}{3}h_{1}^{3}+h_{3}
, if
i
even,

\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{OS}(i+2,2)=\frac{2(2^{i-1}-1)}{3}h_{1}^{3}+h_{1}h_{2}
, if
i
odd,

\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{VG}(i+2,2)=\frac{2(2^{i-1}-1)}{3}h_{1}^{3}+h_{1}e_{2},
if
i
odd.

In particular,

•

\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{2})^{!}_{i},\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{3})^{!}_{i},
are permutation modules, while

•

\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{2})^{!}_{i},\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{3})^{!}_{i}
are permutation modules for all
even

i
, and

•

When
n=2,3
one has

\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{i}\cong\begin{cases}\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{i}&\text{ if }i\text{ is even},\\
\mathrm{sgn}_{n}\otimes\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})^{!}_{i}&\text{ if }i\text{ is odd}.\\
\end{cases}

Sketch of proof.

These all follow by induction on
i
via the recurrences (
100
) and (
87
).
∎

Remark B.5
.

Note the expressions for
n=2
are
consistent with the formula
S(i+1,1)=1
coming from (
110
).
We claim that the expressions for
n=3
are also
consistent with the formulas

(115)

\displaystyle S(i+2,2)

\displaystyle=2^{i+1}-1

(116)

\displaystyle=1+2+2^{2}+\cdots+2^{i}

coming from (
111
), which we illustrate here
for
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{3})_{i}=\mathcal{S}(i+2,2)
.
One can rewrite it as

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}\,\,\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{3})_{i}=\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(i+2,2)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\frac{2^{i+1}+(-1)^{i}}{6}-\frac{1}{2}\right)}_{\text{call this }c_{i}}\cdot h_{1}^{3}+\begin{cases}h_{3}&\text{ if }i\text{ is even},\\
h_{2}h_{1}&\text{ if }i\text{ is odd}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{i+1}}{6}\cdot h_{1}^{3}+\begin{cases}h_{3}-\frac{1}{3}h_{1}^{3}&\text{ if }i\text{ is even},\\
h_{2}h_{1}-\frac{2}{3}h_{1}^{3}&\text{ if }i\text{ is odd}.\end{cases}

Since
h_{1}^{3},h_{2}h_{1},h_{3}

correspond to
\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{3}
-modules of dimensions
6,3,1
, one can check
that this last formula
lifts (
115
).
Interestingly, the number
c_{i}
of copies of the regular representation here (that is,
the coefficient of
h_{1}^{3}
) gives
a sequence
0,0,1,2,5,10,21,42,85,\ldots

for which every other term
0,1,5,21,85,341,\ldots
appears in the Online Encyclopedia of Integer Sequences as
OEIS A002450
.

Expressions lifting (
116
) arise when one uses the
recurrences (
100
) and (
87
),
without trying to rewrite things in terms of
h_{\lambda}
. Recalling that

\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}
is the irreducible

\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-module indexed by
\lambda
,
with
\mathrm{ch}\,\,\mathcal{S}^{\lambda}=s_{\lambda}
, a
Schur function
. One can check that
these recurrences give

(117)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(i+2,2)

\displaystyle=h_{3}+s_{(2,1)}+s_{(2,1)}^{*2}+\cdots+s_{(2,1)}^{*i}.

(118)

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VG}}(i+2,2)

\displaystyle=\omega^{i}(h_{3})+s_{(2,1)}+s_{(2,1)}^{*2}+\cdots+s_{(2,1)}^{*i},

where
\omega:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda

is the involution on symmetric functions
swapping
h_{n}\leftrightarrow e_{n}
for
n\geq 1
, corresponding to tensoring

\mathbb{k}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-modules by the sign character

\mathrm{sgn}_{n}
. Bearing in mind that
h_{3},e_{3}
correspond to
1
-dimensional modules, while
s_{(2,1)}
corresponds to the
2
-dimensional reflection representation
\mathcal{S}^{(2,1)}
of
\mathfrak{S}_{3}
, one sees that (
117
), (
118
) lift (
116
).
Note also that (
117
) is consistent with the
n=3
case of (
61
),
since one has a matroid isomorphism
\operatorname{Br}_{3}\cong U_{2,3}
.

B.4.
The cases
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{4})^{!}
and
\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{4})^{!}

blank line

Here we show that the
\mathfrak{S}_{4}
-modules
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n+3,3)
are permutation modules.
One observes a periodicity in the initial expressions for
f_{n}=\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n+3,3)
below.

(119)

\begin{split}&f_{0}=h_{4}\\
&f_{1}=h_{2}^{2}\\
&f_{2}=h_{1}^{4}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{-h_{2}h_{1}^{2}}}+2h_{2}^{2}+h_{4}\quad\quad=h_{2}[h_{1}^{2}]+h_{1}^{2}h_{2}+h_{4}\\
&f_{3}=4h_{1}^{4}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{-3h_{2}h_{1}^{2}}}+5h_{2}^{2}\quad\qquad\ =2h_{1}^{4}+2h_{2}[h_{1}^{2}]+h_{1}^{2}h_{2}+h_{2}^{2}\\
&f_{4}=14h_{1}^{4}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{-8h_{2}h_{1}^{2}}}+10h_{2}^{2}+h_{4}=10h_{1}^{4}+4h_{2}[h_{1}^{2}]+2h_{2}^{2}+h_{4}\\
&f_{5}=44h_{1}^{4}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{-18h_{2}h_{1}^{2}}}+21h_{2}^{2}\qquad=35h_{1}^{4}+9h_{2}[h_{1}^{2}]+3h_{2}^{2}\\
&f_{6}=135h_{1}^{4}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{-39h_{2}h_{1}^{2}}}+42h_{2}^{2}+h_{4}=115h_{1}^{4}+20h_{2}[h_{1}^{2}]+2h_{2}^{2}+h_{2}h_{1}^{2}+h_{4}\\
&f_{7}=408h_{1}^{4}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{-81h_{2}h_{1}^{2}}}+85h_{2}^{2}\qquad=367h_{1}^{4}+41h_{2}[h_{1}^{2}]+3h_{2}^{2}+h_{2}h_{1}^{2}\end{split}

Proposition B.6
.

The Frobenius characteristic
\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{OS}(n+3,3)=\mathrm{ch}\,\mathrm{OS}^{!}(\operatorname{Br}_{4})_{n}
is
an integer combination of
\{h_{1}^{4},h_{1}^{2}h_{2},h_{2}^{2},h_{4}\}
.

Let
\mathbf{\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{OS}(n+3,3)=a_{n}h_{1}^{4}+b_{n}h_{1}^{2}h_{2}+c^{\prime}_{n}h_{2}^{2}+d_{n}h_{4}},\,n\geq 0
. Let
G_{2}
be the subgroup of order 2 generated by
(12)(34)
.Then

\mathcal{S}_{OS}(n+3,3)
is a permutation module with orbit stabilisers consisting of the wreath product
\mathfrak{S}_{2}[G_{2}]
, as well as a subset of the Young subgroups
\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}
,
\lambda\in\{(1^{4}),(2,1^{2}),(2^{2}),(4)\}
. We have, for

a_{n},c^{\prime}_{n},d_{n}\geq 0
and
b_{n}<0
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{OS}(n+3,3)

(120)

\displaystyle=(a_{n}+\frac{b_{n}}{2})h_{1}^{4}-\frac{b_{n}}{2}h_{2}[h_{1}^{2}]+(c^{\prime}_{n}+b_{n})h_{2}^{2}+d_{n}h_{4},\ n\equiv 0,1\bmod 4,

(121)

\displaystyle=(a_{n}+\frac{b_{n}-1}{2})h_{1}^{4}-\frac{b_{n}-1}{2}h_{2}[h_{1}^{2}]+h_{1}^{2}h_{2}+(c^{\prime}_{n}+b_{n}-1)h_{2}^{2}+d_{n}h_{4},\ n\equiv 2,3\bmod 4.

The coefficients
a_{n},b_{n},c^{\prime}_{n},d_{n}
are determined below.

(1)

The coefficients of
h_{4}
are the sequence
d_{n}=\frac{1+(-1)^{n}}{2},\,n\geq 0.

(2)

The coefficients of
h_{2}^{2}
are
\{0,0,1,2,5,10,21,\ldots\}
, i.e. the numbers
c_{n}
from Remark
B.5
. More precisely,
c^{\prime}_{n}=c_{n+3}=\frac{2^{n+2}-3+(-1)^{n+3}}{6},\,n\geq 0.

(3)

b_{n}-b_{n-1}=-c_{n+2}=-\frac{2^{n+1}-3+(-1)^{n}}{6},n\geq 1,
with
b_{0}=0=b_{1}
. In particular,
b_{n}<0
for
n\geq 2
. We have

b_{n}=-\frac{2}{3}(2^{n}-1)+\frac{n}{2}+\frac{1}{12}(1-(-1)^{n})=-\frac{2^{n+1}}{3}+\frac{n}{2}+\frac{3}{4}-\frac{1}{12}(-1)^{n},\,n\geq 0.

Thus
\mathcal{S}_{OS}(n+3,3)
is NOT
h
-positive for
n\geq 2
.

(4)

The coefficients
a_{n}
are all nonnegative, and strictly positive if
n\geq 2
.
We have
a_{0}=a_{1}=0
and for
n\geq 2
,

a_{n}=\frac{3^{n+1}}{16}-\frac{n}{4}-\frac{4-(-1)^{n}}{16}

In particular,
a_{n}+\frac{b_{n}}{2}
, and
a_{n}+\frac{b_{n}-1}{2}
are positive integers for
n\geq 3
.

Also,
a_{n}
is the multiplicity of the sign representation.

Remark B.7
.

Computing dimensions shows that the
h
-expansion of
\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}(n+3,3)
lifts the formula (
112
) for the Stirling number
S(n+3,3)
.

The coefficient
a_{n}
of
h_{1}^{4}
gives the sequence
0,0,1,4,14,44,135,408,1228,\ldots
, appearing as
OEIS A097137
.
(One checks that
a_{n}-a_{n-2}=(3^{n-1}-1)/2
.) Also the negative of the coefficient
b_{n}
of
h_{1}^{2}h_{2}
gives
0,0,1,3,8,18,39,81,166,336,677,\ldots
, which is
OEIS A011377
or
OEIS A178420
.

A similar analysis for
\mathrm{VG}(\operatorname{Br}_{4})_{i}^{!}
shows that its Frobenius characteristic

\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VG}}(3+i,3)
is also
an integer combination of
\{h_{1}^{4},h_{1}^{2}h_{2},h_{2}^{2},h_{4}\}
, in fact of
\{h_{1}^{4},h_{2}e_{2},h_{4}\}
.

Here is the data for
f_{i}=\mathrm{ch}\,\mathrm{VG}^{!}(4)_{i}
with
0\leq i\leq 11
:

\displaystyle f_{0}

\displaystyle=h_{4}

\displaystyle f_{1}

\displaystyle=h_{2}e_{2}

\displaystyle f_{2}

\displaystyle=h_{1}^{4}+h_{4}

\displaystyle f_{3}

\displaystyle=4\,h_{1}^{4}-\,h_{2}e_{2}

\displaystyle f_{4}

\displaystyle=12\,h_{1}^{4}+2\,h_{2}e_{2}+h_{4}

\displaystyle f_{5}

\displaystyle=40\,h_{1}^{4}+\,h_{2}e_{2}

\displaystyle f_{6}

\displaystyle=127\,h_{1}^{4}-4\,h_{2}e_{2}+h_{4}

\displaystyle f_{7}

\displaystyle=388\,h_{1}^{4}+3\,h_{2}e_{2}

\displaystyle f_{8}

\displaystyle=1186\,h_{1}^{4}+6\,h_{2}e_{2}+h_{4}

\displaystyle f_{9}

\displaystyle=3608\,h_{1}^{4}-11\,h_{2}e_{2}

\displaystyle f_{10}

\displaystyle=10901\,h_{1}^{4}+h_{4}

\displaystyle f_{11}

\displaystyle=32868\,h_{1}^{4}+23\,h_{2}e_{2}

Observe that the set
\{h_{1}^{4},h_{2}e_{2},h_{4}\}
is linearly independent.
One then has the following more precise statement:

Proposition B.8
.

Write
f_{n}
for
\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{VG}(n+3,3)=\mathrm{ch}\,\mathrm{VG}^{!}(\operatorname{Br}_{4})_{n}
. Then

f_{2n-1},f_{2n}-h_{4}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[h_{1}^{4},h_{2}e_{2}]

and hence for
n\geq 0
, both the representation
\mathcal{S}_{VG}(2n+2,3)
and the quotient representation
\mathcal{S}_{VG}(2n+3,3)/\mathbb{1}_{\mathfrak{S}_{4}}
are fixed under tensoring
with the sign representation
\mathrm{sgn}
of
\mathfrak{S}_{4}
.

Let
f_{n}=a_{n}h_{1}^{4}+b_{n}h_{2}e_{2}+d_{n}h_{4}.
Then, with initial values
a_{0}=a_{1}=0,a_{2}=1,a_{3}=4
,
b_{0}=0,b_{1}=1,b_{2}=0,b_{3}=-1
, one has that
d_{n}=\frac{1+(-1)^{n}}{2}
,
a_{n}\geq 0
for all
n\geq 0
, and for
n\geq 3
:

\displaystyle a_{n}

\displaystyle=6a_{n-1}-11a_{n-2}+6a_{n-3}+2(b_{n-1}-b_{n-2}+b_{n-3})-d_{n-2},

\displaystyle b_{n}

\displaystyle=-b_{n-2}+2b_{n-3}.

The sequence
\{b_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
appears in
OEIS A077912
, with generating function
\frac{x}{1+x^{2}-2x^{3}}
.

Moreover
\mathcal{S}_{VG}(n+3,3)
is a permutation module if and only if
b_{n}=0
or
b_{n}\leq-2
. Write
\ -b_{n}=2\alpha_{n}+3\beta_{n}
for nonnegative integers
\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}
. Then
a_{n}-(\alpha_{n}+\beta_{n})
is nonnegative and

f_{n}=(a_{n}-(\alpha_{n}+\beta_{n}))h_{1}^{4}+\alpha_{n}\,\mathrm{ch}\,(1\big\uparrow_{G_{2}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{4}})+\beta_{n}\,\mathrm{ch}\,(1\big\uparrow_{V_{4}}^{\mathfrak{S}_{4}})+d_{n}h_{4}

is the Frobenius characteristic of a permutation module,
where the orbit stabilisers are
\mathfrak{S}_{1}
,
\mathfrak{S}_{4}
and the subgroups
G_{2}=\langle(12)(34)\rangle
and
V_{4}=\{(1),(12)(34),(13)(24),(14)(23)\}
of
\mathfrak{S}_{4}
.

B.5.
The case
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{5})^{!}

blank line

In this section we show that the
\mathfrak{S}_{5}
-modules
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n+4,4)
are also permutation modules. We also show that the
h
-expansions exhibit a curious periodicity modulo 4.

The initial expressions for
f_{n}=\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n+4,4)
are as follows.

(122)

\begin{split}f_{0}&=h_{5},\ f_{1}=h_{3}h_{2},\\
f_{2}&=h_{4}h_{1}+h_{2}h_{1}^{3}+2h_{3}h_{2}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{-h_{3}h_{1}^{2}}}=h_{1}h_{2}[h_{2}]+h_{2}(h_{3}+e_{3})+h_{2}^{2}h_{1},\\
f_{3}&=2h_{1}^{5}+3h_{2}^{2}h_{1}+2h_{3}h_{2},\\
f_{4}&=12h_{1}^{5}+8h_{2}^{2}h_{1}+2h_{3}h_{2}+h_{5}\\
f_{5}&=60h_{1}^{5}+18h_{2}^{2}h_{1}+3h_{3}h_{2}\\
f_{6}&=274h_{1}^{5}+38h_{2}^{2}h_{1}+h_{2}h_{1}^{3}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathbf{-h_{3}h_{1}^{2}}}+4h_{3}h_{2}+h_{4}h_{1}\\
&=(274h_{1}^{5}+38h_{2}h_{1}^{2}+2h_{3}h_{2})+f_{2}\\
f_{7}&=1194h_{1}^{5}+81h_{2}^{2}h_{1}+4h_{3}h_{2}.\end{split}

Proposition B.9
.

The
\mathfrak{S}_{5}
-module
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n+4,4)=\mathrm{ch}\,\mathrm{OS}^{!}(\operatorname{Br}_{5})_{n}

is a permutation module for all
n\geq 0
, with orbit stabilisers given by

•

the Young subgroups
\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}
for
\lambda\in\{(1^{5}),(2^{2},1),(3,2)\}
if
\mathbf{n\equiv 1,3\bmod 4}.

•

the Young subgroup
\mathfrak{S}_{(2^{2},1)}
, as well as the subgroups

\mathfrak{S}_{1}\times I_{2}(4),A_{3}\times\mathfrak{S}_{2}
if
\mathbf{n\equiv 2\bmod 4}.

Here
A_{3}\times\mathfrak{S}_{2}
is the subgroup of the Young subgroup
\mathfrak{S}_{3}\times\mathfrak{S}_{2}
, for the alternating subgroup
A_{3}
of
\mathfrak{S}_{3}
.

•

the Young subgroups
\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda}
for

\lambda\in\{(1^{5}),(2^{2},1),(3,2),(5)\}
if
\mathbf{n\equiv 0\bmod 4}.

Let
J=\{h_{1}^{5},h_{2}^{2}h_{1},h_{3}h_{2}\}
. Let

f_{n}=\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{OS}(n+4,4)=\mathrm{ch}\,A^{!}(5)_{n}
.
Then

(1)

f_{n}
is a nonnegative integer combination of the set
J
if
n\equiv 1,3\bmod 4.

(2)

f_{n}-f_{2}
is a nonnegative integer combination of
J
if
n\equiv 2\bmod 4.

(3)

f_{n}-f_{0}
is a nonnegative integer combination of
J
if
n\equiv 0\bmod 4.

The following explicit decomposition holds for
f_{n+4}-f_{n}
:

(123)

f_{n+4}-f_{n}=a_{n}h_{1}^{5}+b_{n}h_{1}h_{2}^{2}+2h_{2}h_{3},\ n\geq 0,

where
b_{0}=8
,
b_{n}=10(2^{n})-2,\,n\geq 1,
and

(124)

a_{n}=\frac{1}{3}(1+17\cdot 4^{n+1}-3\cdot 2^{n+1}-3^{n+3}).

Let
0\leq i\leq 3
and
k\geq 0
. Then

(125)

\begin{split}&f_{4k+4+i}-f_{i}=\alpha_{k,i}h_{1}^{5}+\beta_{k,i}h_{2}^{2}h_{1}+2(k+1)h_{2}h_{3}\\
&\text{where}\\
&\alpha_{k,i}=\frac{k+1}{3}+4^{i+1}\frac{256^{k+1}-1}{45}-3^{i+2}\frac{81^{k+1}-1}{80}-2^{i+1}\frac{16^{k+1}-1}{15},\\
&\beta_{k,i}=2^{i+1}\frac{16^{k+1}-1}{3}-2(k+1).\end{split}

The multiplicity of the sign representation in
f_{n}
is

\begin{cases}\alpha_{k,i},&n=4(k+1)+i\text{ and }k\geq 0,\\
2,&n=3,\\
0,&n<3.\end{cases}

Remark B.10
.

(
The restriction of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n+1,n-1)
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VG}}(n+1,n-1)
to
\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}
)

Observe that in each of the cases
\mathrm{OS}(\operatorname{Br}_{n})
,
1\leq n\leq 5
, the restriction of the
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
-module to
\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}
is always an
h
-positive permutation module.
The restriction is not
h
-positive for
\mathcal{S}(n+1,n-1)
when
n\geq 5
, although the following formula shows that it is a permutation module.

\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}(n+1,n-1)\downarrow^{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}_{\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}}=(h_{n-2}h_{1}+h_{n-3}h_{2}+2h_{n-3}h_{1}^{2}+h_{n-4}h_{1}^{3}+h_{n-4}h_{3})\delta_{n\geq 4}+h_{n-5}\,\mathrm{ch}\,\mathbb{1}\uparrow_{I_{2}(4)}^{\mathfrak{S}_{4}}\delta_{n\geq 5}.

Here
I_{2}(4)
is the dihedral group of order 8.

Remark B.11
.

(
The restriction of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n+3,3)
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VG}}(n+3,3)
)
With the coefficients defined in Proposition
B.6
, the restriction of
\mathcal{S}_{OS}(n+3,3)
to
\mathfrak{S}_{3}
has Frobenius characteristic

(4a_{n}+b_{n})h_{1}^{3}+2(b_{n}+c^{\prime}_{n})h_{1}h_{2}+d_{n}h_{3},

and is thus
h
-positive. In particular

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n+3,3)\big\downarrow_{\mathfrak{S}_{3}}
is a permutation module whose point stabilisers are Young subgroups.

Proposition
B.8
shows that a similar statement holds for
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{VG}}(n+3,3)\big\downarrow_{\mathfrak{S}_{3}}
; here the orbit stabilisers are
\mathfrak{S}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{S}_{3}
.

Remark B.12
.

(
The restriction of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n+4,4)
)
The restriction of
f_{n}=\mathrm{ch}\,\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n+4,4)
to
\mathfrak{S}_{4}
is
h
-positive,
supported on the set
\{h_{1}^{4},h_{1}^{2}h_{2},h_{2}^{2}\}
if
n\equiv 1,3\bmod 4
, the set
\{h_{1}^{4},h_{1}^{2}h_{2},h_{2}^{2},h_{4}\}
if
n\equiv 0\bmod 4
, and finally the set

\{h_{1}^{4},h_{1}^{2}h_{2},h_{2}^{2},h_{3}h_{1},h_{4}\}
if
n\equiv 2\bmod 4
. In particular

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n+4,4)\big\downarrow_{\mathfrak{S}_{4}}
is a permutation module whose point stabilisers are Young subgroups.

Remark B.13
.

(
The multiplicity of the trivial representation
) Here we collect formulas for the multiplicity of the trivial representation:

For
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n+1,n-1)
, the multiplicity of the trivial representation is 3 for
n\geq 4
, and the multiplicity of the sign representation is 0 for
n\neq 3,4
, and 1 otherwise.

For
\mathcal{S}_{OS}(n+2,2)
, the multiplicity is

\frac{2^{n+1}}{6}+\frac{3+(-1)^{n}}{6}.

For
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n+3,3)
, the multiplicity of the trivial representation is

\frac{3^{n+1}}{16}+\frac{n}{4}+\frac{8+5(-1)^{n}}{16},

giving
\{1,1,3,6,17,47,139,412,\ldots\}
.

For
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{OS}}(n+4,4)
, (with definitions as in Proposition
B.9
), the multiplicity of the trivial representation is

\frac{k+1}{3}+4^{i+1}\frac{256^{k+1}-1}{45}-3^{i+2}\frac{81^{k+1}-1}{80}+2^{i+3}\frac{16^{k+1}-1}{5}+\langle f_{i},h_{5}\rangle.
